OpenAI valued at $1.25 trillion, as rival Anthropic nears its IPO

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

CEO Sam Altman said recently that OpenAI is prioritizing safety instead of going ‘guns blazing’ toward an IPO.﻿

CEO Sam Altman said recently that OpenAI is prioritizing safety instead of going ‘guns blazing’ toward an IPO.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

OpenAI is now valued at $1.25 trillion in a new round of funding that has raised another $11 billion, according to Bloomberg. That would make it one of the world’s most valuable companies, not long after raising $122 billion from investors in March at a valuation of $852 billion.

Echoing FT and Wall Street Journal reports from last week, Bloomberg says OpenAI is still pursuing additional investments that could increase the amount to $20 billion, and that it had initially hoped to raise as much as $30 billion at a higher valuation of $1.4 trillion. That was before tech stocks dropped, and the results of the midterms showed how much public opinion has turned against AI companies and data center projects.

OpenAI’s rival Anthropic filed its S-1 to go public last week. Earlier this year, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said it’s holding off on its own IPO until it can make more confident claims about its models’ safety. He didn’t give a specific timeline for the IPO, but said at the time, “I think it’s also kind of bad for the world if OpenAI waits too long to go public.”

OpenAI reportedly raises $11B in new funding at $1.25T valuation

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

OpenAI Group PBC has reportedly raised $11 billion as part of a funding round that could nearly double in size over the coming weeks, as sources told Bloomberg that investors wired the funds to the company today.

The tipsters didn’t name the backers. The list of possible participants includes Microsoft Corp., which contributed to at least four of OpenAI’s previous rounds. That included its most recent $122 billion raise in March, which also drew the participation of Nvidia Corp., Amazon.com Inc. and about two dozen others.

It’s believed that OpenAI could raise up to $9 billion in additional funding as part of the round. The deal is expected to value the company at $1.25 billion. That potentially puts it on track to surpass Anthropic PBC’s market capitalization.

Anthropic hopes to achieve a valuation of $1.5 trillion in its upcoming initial public offering. However, several of its investors reportedly don’t consider that to be a realistic goal. According to the Wall Street Journal, one shareholder reportedly suggested that the company pursue a valuation closer to $1 trillion.

Anthropic’s IPO filing revealed that its quarterly revenue jumped 1,127% on a year-over-year basis in the third quarter. Last month, the Financial Times reported that OpenAI closed the quarter with $50 billion in net annualized revenue, which represented a 77% year-over-year increase. The company’s growth was driven by its enterprise business, which saw sales more than double.

The latest addition to OpenAI’s enterprise portfolio debuted on Thursday. GPT-6 Cyber can perform most cybersecurity tasks better than GPT-6 Astra, the company’s most capable general-purpose model. OpenAI engineers used it to develop 948 Linux⁠ exploits and uncover 81 high-severity zero-day flaws in Firefox.

OpenAI President Greg Brockman revealed on Wednesday that the company had experienced a slowdown in the first half of the year. Its growth started accelerating after the June launch of GPT-5.6 Sol, the immediate predecessor to GPT-6 Astra. Brockman added that OpenAI’s ChatGPT Work productivity application and coding tools played a role as well.

The company plans to go public sometime in 2027. Today’s cash infusion should make it easier for OpenAI to finance its steep infrastructure expenses until the listing.

In July, The Information reported that the ChatGPT developer has more than $665 billion worth of chip and data center commitments. OpenAI has developed a custom inference accelerator in collaboration with Broadcom Inc. as part of an effort to lower its long-term hardware spending.

Anthropic, for its part, is expected to kick off its IPO roadmap next week. The company reportedly plans to go public before Thanksgiving.

Lincoln Celebrated the Art of Rejuvenation at Billboard Latin Music Week

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

From wellness retreats to sensory experiences, Lincoln invited attendees to recharge amid the excitement of Miami

Miami doesn’t exactly do quiet—especially during Billboard Latin Music Week, when the city comes alive with music, industry conversations and nonstop events. But amid the excitement, Lincoln offered attendees something unexpected: a moment to slow down and recharge. Explore Billboard See latest videos, charts and news

Throughout the week at Faena Forum, the luxury automotive brand brought its signature approach to comfort, design and rejuvenation to life through immersive experiences that offered a glimpse into the thoughtful details behind Lincoln’s latest innovations. Related Queens of the Stone Age Crowned on ARIA Chart With 'Perfecth' Lil Baby Brings 'Fresh' to Life: Stream It Now Lil Baby, JENNIE, Dove Cameron, XG, Boys Noize & More: New Music Friday Guide

Between back-to-back panels, a little downtime went a long way. Lincoln’s Rejuvenation Pod invited attendees to step away from the bustle and settle into a moment of calm. Inspired by the Lincoln Rejuvenate feature available in the Nautilus and Navigator, the experience offered a taste of relaxation designed to be enjoyed behind the wheel.

Just a few steps away, the Lincoln Navigator turned heads in a specialty exterior color, Frosted Fig. Guests explored the SUV’s luxurious interior, complete with an expansive panoramic sunroof and a sweeping digital display across the dashboard. And because Miami’s sunshine rarely takes a day off, Lincoln offered ice pops to help guests beat the heat.

Inside Faena Forum, the experience continued with opportunities to indulge the senses. A lush floral installation became a picture-perfect backdrop for attendees capturing memories from the week, while a nearby scent discovery station invited guests to explore the fragrance options available through the Digital Scent feature. Together, the installations offered a reminder that luxury isn’t always about what catches the eye and sometimes, it’s about how a space makes you feel.

The Lincoln experience extended beyond Faena Forum on Monday evening, as Billboard’s Latin Power Players celebration brought together some of the industry’s most influential names. Select honorees enjoyed courtesy rides in Lincoln vehicles to the awards ceremony, arriving in style for a night celebrating those shaping Latin music’s global impact.

Across a week defined by music, movement and milestone moments, Lincoln made the spaces in between feel just as memorable. From discovering new scents to exploring luxurious interiors and finding a few minutes to recharge, each experience reflected the pleasure of being present.

And in a city that rarely slows its rhythm, Lincoln made a compelling case for enjoying the pause.

The full release will feature new classes, finished campaign, a new league, and more.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Path of Exile 2's full release is out on December 11, and coming with it are two new classes, two new acts, a new league, tons of new monsters, bosses, battles, endgame changes, gem updates, atlas and skill tree updates, a new pinnacle boss, base types, uniques, and, I don't know, probably a kitchen sink or something. Let's go over it.

First of all, we'll have two new classes: the Shadow and the Duelist. With them, we'll get all new skill gems for daggers and swords, with tons of new builds available. Daggers introduce new mechanics around stealth, traps, shadow skills with clones that attack with you, and classic skills like blade vortex and fire trap.

The Duelist will introduce swords, and with it fan favorite skills like cyclone and hemorrhage. With swords we'll be able to use stances like blood and sand or ready stance. Add to this being able to two-hand, dual wield, or sword and board and swords look to add a huge amount of mechanical complexity.

After two years of early access, the campaign will finally be fully finished. Acts five and six will complete our story of chasing the Beast through the world, with a return to Oriath and beyond. The campaign will also feature new bespoke rare monsters with their own abilities, appearances, and even mods. In addition, the campaign will feature an introduction to the league mechanic for 1.0, Conquerors of Wraeclast.

The league will feature a base building mechanic, where you recruit armies and build up a castle. As you progress through the campaign, you'll recruit armies to battle against enemy armies. There are over 70 points of interest throughout the campaign that let you recruit new types of soldiers for your army, and battle through to kill the boss and get loot.

As you progress your base, you can recruit commanders that will be able to take your recruits and fight battles for you. Like Kingsmarch in Path of Exile 1, these commanders can run maps for you, letting you convert your gold into progress through the atlas and lots of loot. Your castle gets upgraded just like Heroes of Might and Magic, with a beautiful base that you can spend gold to upgrade.

After all the pinnacle bosses and campaign, you can use your armies to battle against other players. Depending on which of the three factions you choose to join (Karui, Ezomyte, or Marraketh), you can battle to take control of various castles around the atlas. As long as your faction controls a castle, you can travel to it on the Atlas to skip around and get to sweet new maps, and you'll receive additional item rarity. It also unlocks hunting grounds nearby that feature rare monsters with increased loot. You'll be able to use currency you find in battle to craft gear, a system which lets you pick mods to remove and reroll into new fractured mods.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

All of the new campaign areas feature new bosses, which you'll also face in the endgame in over 25 new maps.

The endgame has been updated as well—in addition to being able to use castles to jump around the atlas, the atlas skill tree has been substantially changed. Instead of 300+ nodes, it's been trimmed way down to about 70. If you beeline the skills, you should end up with about 50 of 70 by the time you battle against the Arbiter of Divinity.

Tons of gems have been updated. Not only are we getting new gems for daggers and swords, but they've added new skills for existing weapons as well. Support gems have been overhauled so that they're more useful as well, like all of the different elemental penetration gems being rolled into one called elemental penetration. Support gems like brutality are also better, with less of a penalty for chaos damage. Chain support becomes just an upgrade, without the penalty to damage on the initial hit.

The passive tree has had huge changes. In addition to a bunch of new nodes, they've changed how jewels work. Instead of always being located on the travel nodes on the outside of the tree, they'll be located in actual skill clusters. Hopefully this will prevent all of our endgame skill trees looking the same, with a big circle maximizing jewel slots and nothing else.

With tons of new items including base types and uniques, there will be lots of new loot to find. Some of the base types look insanely powerful, like an armor that makes you immune to curses. They've overhauled over 70 uniques and added about 40 more, which should make finding uniques way more exciting, especially in the campaign.

Taken all together, 1.0 looks like it will be an incredible moment for Path of Exile 2, both for players who've been waiting to play it, and for those of us who already have hundreds or thousands of hours in the ARPG. The full release of Path of Exile 2 will be available on December 11.

After a coordinated influence campaign to bamboozle his parents into getting a high speed internet connection to play EverQuest, his fate was well and truly sealed. When he's not writing about videogames, he's teaching karate, cooking an overly complicated dish, or attempting to raise his daughter with a well rounded classical education (Civilization, Doom, and Baldur's Gate, of course). He's probably mapping in Path of Exile right now.

Methane emissions stayed stubbornly high despite a federal push to cut them

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Satellite data show U.S. oil and gas methane emissions stayed nearly flat from 2019 to 2024, even as production grew and federal policies shifted.

Congress created the first federal methane fee in 2022 to encourage oil and gas companies to reduce emissions.

Satellite measurements now show that methane emissions from the sector remained near 11 million tons (10 million metric tons) a year from 2019 to 2024, with a slight increase in the final year.

The findings reveal no detectable decline following the law’s passage, though much of its enforcement framework had yet to take effect.

Methane emissions per unit of oil and gas produced fell by about 11% over the six years.

The researchers linked that decline mainly to new pipelines, better equipment, and earlier leak rules.

Ao Chen, a doctoral student at Johns Hopkins University, led the work with Scot Miller, an associate professor of environmental health and engineering there.

Colleagues at the University of Texas at Austin and McGill University took part.

Methane is nearly 30 times as potent as carbon dioxide at trapping heat, though it lasts a shorter time in the air. That makes it the second most important greenhouse gas to cut, Miller said.

Two instruments in orbit, Europe’s TROPOMI and Japan’s GOSAT, measure how much of it sits in the air below them.

The team fed roughly 2,000 of those readings a day over the lower 48 states into a model of how wind moves gas around. Then they ran it backward to work out where the methane came from.

The result is a map of emissions in boxes about 35 miles (55 kilometers) on a side, one for every day from 2019 to 2024.

Deciding how much of each box’s methane came from oil and gas rather than cattle or wetlands relies on existing inventories.

The team’s eight model runs show that choice can shift the oil and gas total by up to 20%.

The satellite totals ran about 17% above the EPA’s own inventory from 2019 to 2022. Earlier aerial and satellite studies have found the same gap, and some found it far wider.

Oil and gas methane across the lower 48 stayed between 10.3 million and 11.5 million tons (9.3 to 10.4 million metric tons) every year of the study.

It fell by about 640,000 tons (580,000 metric tons) from 2019 to 2023, then rose by about 230,000 tons (210,000 metric tons) in 2024.

Production, meanwhile, grew 8.5% over the same six years, so the methane released per barrel of oil and gas fell by 11.1%, or about 2.2% a year.

Most of that drop came between 2020 and 2022, before any new federal rule took hold.

“The good news is that emissions have not markedly increased, as many scientists feared would happen after an enormous increase in natural gas production in the decades following the hydraulic fracturing boom,” he said.

“The bad news is that oil and gas production is still a large percentage of U.S. methane emissions, and we do not find any evidence that a flurry of federal legislation had an impact on emissions.”

The Inflation Reduction Act created the Waste Emissions Charge, a fee on methane from large oil and gas facilities that was to begin in 2024.

It also set aside $1.36 billion to help companies find and fix leaks. The EPA finalized its own methane rules for new and existing wells in 2024.

Much of that framework had yet to take effect during the study.

The fee’s details stayed unsettled, and the rules for existing facilities gave states until 2026 to file their plans. Then Congress voided the fee rule in early 2025.

The EPA had projected the two measures together would cut 2024 emissions by about 420,000 tons (380,000 metric tons). The satellites saw an increase instead.

“We don’t see a detectable decrease in emissions after the Inflation Reduction Act,” Chen said.

“Of course, there’s no way to test the counterfactual. It’s possible that without the Inflation Reduction Act, methane emissions would be much higher.”

The biggest drop came from the biggest emitter.

Texas oil and gas methane fell 14%, from about 3.9 million tons (3.5 million metric tons) in 2019 to 3.3 million tons (3.0 million metric tons) in 2024. The state has no climate target.

The Gulf Coast Express line opened in late 2019, and the Whistler and Matterhorn Express lines followed, carrying gas from the Permian Basin to the coast and to Houston.

Pipeline access can allow producers to sell gas that might otherwise be flared or vented. Rising exports of liquefied natural gas made that gas worth more.

States with climate targets, among them California, Colorado, and New Mexico, cut oil and gas methane at rates that overlapped with states that have none.

Their inventories were more accurate, though. The satellite estimate was 3.6 times Texas’s official count and 5.8 times Oklahoma’s, while New Mexico’s matched.

California has pledged to cut its total methane 40% below 2013 levels by 2030.

That means cutting roughly 560,000 tons (510,000 metric tons) in four years, against about 130,000 tons (120,000 metric tons) over the past ten.

Colorado’s case is murkier. Its oil and gas methane swings by more than 30% from one year to the next, wider than the 26% cut its 2025 target demands.

That variability makes progress toward the target difficult to assess.

The study cannot establish what emissions would have been without the federal measures.

Continued satellite monitoring will help researchers track whether future policies and industry changes produce sustained reductions.

The full study was published in the journal Communications Earth & Environment.

San Diego still have things to play for after missing out on postseason, says Varas

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Mikey Varas was thrilled with how San Diego FC played in their win over St. Louis City SC last weekend and insists they still have things to play for against Sporting Kansas City.

After a stunning success in their expansion season, San Diego will not be playing in the MLS playoffs this time around, as they sit 13th in the Western Conference on 38 points, five adrift of ninth place.

In fact, there is nothing riding on this game for either team, though San Diego are trying to finish the season on a high, having ended their 10-game winless streak with their 1-0 victory over St. Louis.

And Varas is keen to give the fans something to cheer about on Decision Day and build on what they did last weekend.

"[The win over St. Louis] shows the character of the group," he said.

"There's still a lot to play for, first and foremost the honour of the city and the fans, and secondly, because it's our duty as competitors.

"We also want to start winning next season right now, and the last thing is out of personal pride for ourselves.

"The boys showed a ton of fight and a ton of quality against a phenomenal opponent and a team that's well coached."

— San Diego FC (@sandiegofc) November 3, 2026

Sporting KC were ruled out of the playoffs quite some time ago as they prop up the West, sitting 15th with only 23 points.

They are currently on a seven-game winless streak, though their last victory did come on the road, over the Houston Dynamo back on September 26.

Their biggest issue has been their defence, having conceded 81 goals this term, though head coach Raphael Wicky believes there have been signs of improvement.

"There are still moments where we can play a little bit and where we have to be less sloppy and lose fewer easy balls to then give the other team these moments," he said after a 1-1 draw with FC Dallas.

"But I believe there is a group getting together and, I have said this a few times now, I strongly believe if this group we have is at one point fully healthy and fit, with some other players who will join next year, there will be a competitive team. I believe strongly in that."

San Diego's lone goal against St. Louis came from Dreyer, who is their leading scorer this season with 15.

It is Dreyer's second straight season with 15+ goals (19 in 2025), with no other San Diego player netting more than 12 in a single season.

Sporting KC have allowed 81 goals this season, the most in a single campaign in MLS history.

Sporting's current goal differential of -42 would be the second-lowest in a single season in MLS history, besting only FC Cincinnati's 2019 season (-44), and Cleveland will be determined not to make unwanted history.

— Sporting Kansas City (@SportingKC) November 4, 2026

San Diego have never lost to Sporting KC in three all-time meetings (W2 D1) and have never conceded a goal against them.

It is the only time San Diego have kept three straight clean sheets against any opponent across all competitions.

Varas' side secured a 1-0 win over St. Louis to end a run of 10 straight games without a win (D5 L5). They won their first three MLS matches this season, but have won just six of 30 since then (D11 L13).

Sporting's 1-1 draw with Dallas extended their winless streak to seven games (D2 L5). It was Kansas City's 32nd MLS match allowing a goal this season, which ties the most matches conceding a goal in a single regular season by any team in MLS history.

Moyes relieved to see Everton make the best of unconvincing display

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Though David Moyes felt Everton's performance against Coventry City was not entirely convincing, he was enthused by the way they fought back to win 3-1.

Thierno Barry scored twice and provided an assist for Kiernan Dewsbury-Hall as the Toffees recovered after falling behind to Yann Gboho's opener at Hill Dickinson Stadium.

Everton have now won three straight games in all competitions, the last two of which have been in the Premier League, ahead of the international break.

It was just the third instance of Everton going 1-0 down in the second half of a Premier League game before coming back to win by more than one goal, along with 3-1 wins over West Ham in November 2008 and Burnley in September 2021.

The victory came on a landmark occasion for Moyes, as he oversaw his 592nd game across two stints in charge of Everton – that is the joint-most of any manager in the club's history, alongside two-time First Division title-winner Harry Catterick.

Moyes still felt there was plenty his side could have done better, though, after they only managed one shot more than their opponents (15 to 14) and gave up three Opta-defined big chances.

"I wasn't quite so sure about it being a good performance. We got a result, but I have to say Coventry made it really difficult for us," Moyes told BBC Match of the Day.

"You can see why they have picked up some really good results. They won at Nottingham Forest and beat Sunderland the other week.

"They had a couple of decisions that might have gone in their favour tonight. But in the end, we got the goals that mattered, and when you win in the Premier League you have to take it, because it is so hard to get the wins."

10  3 Thierno Barry szn.  https://t.co/3NNeZz7UcG

But Moyes was delighted to see Barry continue his prolific start to the season, with the Frenchman's brace taking him to 10 goals in 13 outings this campaign.

That is already more goals than he netted in 41 matches across all competitions last season (eight), and Manchester City's Erling Haaland (14) is the only Premier League player to outscore him in 2026-27.

"We gave away a really poor goal but the reaction from there was really good. It was important that Thierno Barry got another couple of goals tonight, which was huge for us," Moyes said.

"We've always liked him, and we want him to keep scoring. He's missed quite a few chances, but tonight he got important goals for us.

"He's becoming an important piece for us and he's got 10 goals, which is really good."

Everton's first game back after the international break is away to Brentford on November 23, before they host Liverpool in the Merseyside derby six days later.

The (Wild) Story Behind How The Rocky Statue Actually Caused Problems For I Play Rocky

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

The Scene In You Can See Everything That Showed EVERYTHING Might NOT Have Been Billy Evans' Idea

You Can See Everything Is Finally Number One At The Box Office (And It Also Hit A Rare Milestone)

I'm Shocked To Learn One Surprising Scene In You Can See Everything Came From Billy Evans Himself

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The statue that pays homage to Rocky got in another Rocky homage's way.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I would bet that there’s a large group of people who think about Philadelphia and immediately picture Rocky running up the steps of the Philadelphia Museum of Art. I know I do. I mean, Rocky is so synonymous with Philly that there’s a statue of Sylvester Stallone’s character at the actual steps he ran up. Now, that’s all fine, dandy and a great way to pay homage to one of the best sports movies. However, when it came time to make I Play Rocky, a movie about the making of Rocky, that statue actually caused problems.

Now, famously, Rocky was a hard film to make, and that’s shown in I Play Rocky. However, this project on the 2026 movie schedule had its own set of problems to deal with too. For example, when it came time to recreate the legendary moment where Rocky runs up the steps, the statue of Rocky literally got in their way, as director Peter Farrelly told me during an interview for CinemaBlend:

Well, there's a statue of Rocky right where he has to go to, and the museum, for some reason, was like, ‘No.’ We ask, ‘Can we take it away? And we'll move it back, and we'll get it insured, and we'll do everything.’ They're like, ‘No.’ I think, ‘Well, we're shooting there, you know, we can't have the statue.’ They're like, ‘Tough.’ That was a problem.

What’s ironic about all this is that a replica of the Rocky statue only made its way to the top of the steps in December 2024, per NBC Philadelphia. Then, as CBS News reported at the start of 2026, the Philadelphia Art Commission approved the relocation of the original statue that sat at the bottom of the stairs to the top. The original statue was finally put there this fall.

So, along with this movie having to ask to get a statue moved, there was just a lot of talk about moving generally going on around the same time period.

Anyway, in the case of I Play Rocky, they wanted to get the statue temporarily moved because obviously it was not there when Sylvester Stallone ran up the steps the first time while filming Rocky in the ‘70s. So, they did some digging and realized someone Farrelly knew had connections at the Philadelphia Museum of Art, as he told me:

And then somebody looked on the board of trustees for the museum, and Brian Roberts' name was on there. Brian Roberts owns Comcast, that's based out of Philadelphia, and Comcast owns Universal, the studio. But this isn't a Universal movie, and I know Brian. I'd met him a couple of times. We did Green Book at Universal, and so I called Brian and said, ‘Hey, these people are being difficult. Can you? Is there anything you could do?’ He goes, ‘I'll look into it.’

Brian L. Roberts, the chairman and CEO of Comcast, is a longtime supporter of the museum, and Comcast’s co-founder, the late Julian Brodsky, was a trustee. Clearly, Farrelly’s connection to Roberts turned out to be very useful. That’s because the morning after the director called him, the statue was gone:

And then the next morning we got there, and the statue was gone. So, yeah, he helped out. I like giving him credit. We gave him a thanks at the end of the movie, even though it's not his movie.

I can say with certainty that the statue is not in the movie, as you can see when Anthony Ippolito’s Sylvester Stallone runs to the top of the steps and does the iconic pose you see in Rocky. And it’s entertaining to think about how this statue, which was put there to honor Rocky, had to be moved so this movie could honor Rocky.

Now, if you’d like to see the film that inspired the iconic statue at the Rocky Steps, you can stream Rocky with a Paramount+ subscription. If you’d like to see a movie about the making of Rocky (sans the statue), you can catch the well-reviewed I Play Rocky in select theaters right now and everywhere on November 20.

Riley Utley is the Weekend Editor at CinemaBlend. She has written for national publications as well as daily and alt-weekly newspapers in Spokane, Washington, Syracuse, New York and Charleston, South Carolina. She graduated with her master’s degree in arts journalism and communications from the Newhouse School at Syracuse University. Since joining the CB team she has covered numerous TV shows and movies -- including her personal favorite shows Ted Lasso and The Marvelous Mrs. Maisel. She also has followed and consistently written about everything from Taylor Swift to Fire Country, and she's enjoyed every second of it.

How to get the Piercing Gaze Hunter Mask in The Division 2: Echoes of Central Park

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Piercing Gaze Hunter mask is a secret item you can unearth in The Division 2, but you’ll need to gain access to the Echoes of Central Park DLC. Then, you’ll have to solve a set of clues and recover few letters to get your hands on this mask.

Let’s find out how to get this secret gear added to your in-game collection.

First, you have to head to the location shown on the image below. Go to Lincoln Square district, and the spot will have a wall covered in leaves. It’s not difficult to find, and you’ll notice a bunch of text saying “Break my Walls”.

Shoot the wall, and you’ll find letters on it. Keep shooting until all the letters are visible. This is the first location where you find the letters. However, there’s a second location where you’ll find more of these letters.

Head to the marked spot on the image above, and you’ll find a second wall with the text “Break my Walls” written on it. Again, start shooting until you get three different letters.

Once the parts mentioned above have been solved, go to the southern part of the Lincoln Square District. You’ll find a roof with plant pots and statue, and you need to destroy all of them.

Some of these pots will be indestructible, and you need to keep shooting at them. This will eventually summon the Hunter.

To get your mask, you’ll have to defeat the Hunter as it’s the only way. As long as you stay clear from the Seeker Mines, you shouldn’t have any problem defeating the Hunter and obtaining the secret mask.

Dragon Age writer is "collaborating" on Mass Effect-like RPG The Expanse: Osiris Reborn

"The Expanse has long been one of my favourite series"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

David Gaider is one of BioWare's most notable writers. After cutting his teeth on Baldur's Gate 2, he wrote some of Star Wars: Knights of the Old Republic's most memorable characters, and would go on to lead the narrative for Dragon Age: Origins, setting the tone for BioWare's original fantasy franchise. Gaider notably never worked on Mass Effect, but he's getting the chance to channel its spirit as part of the writing team for The Expanse: Osiris Reborn.

"Alongside my work at Summerfall, I've also been collaborating with the Owlcat Games team on The Expanse: Osiris Reborn, focusing on certain aspects of the game's narrative," Gaider says in a Bluesky post. "The Expanse has long been one of my favourite series, and so obviously I jumped at the opportunity to be involved."

It's tough not to think about Mass Effect when looking at Osiris Reborn – after all, it too is a sci-fi RPG that controls like a third-person shooter where you build relationships with a cast of companions. The connection became even more direct last month, when developer Owlcat threw its own support behind the Save Mass Effect movement.

Gaider's involvement certainly adds to the bona fides of Osiris Reborn, and Owlcat's increasingly successful history with old-school CRPG throwbacks like Pathfinder: Kingmaker, Wrath of the Righteous, and Warhammer 40,000: Rogue Trader suggests that the sci-fi RPG could be a breakout. A beta released earlier this year was janky but promising, and the developer has been proactive about responding to feedback ahead of the game's planned release next year.

For details on that other big Mass Effect-like, check out our guide to Exodus.

He's been covering games (with occasional dalliances in the worlds of anime and pro wrestling) since 2015, first as a freelancer, then as a news writer at PCGamesN for nearly five years. His love for games was sparked somewhere between Metal Gear Solid 2 and Knights of the Old Republic, and these days you can usually find him splitting his entertainment time between retro gaming, the latest big action-adventure title, or a long haul in American Truck Simulator.

How the Newly Elected Congress Can Save the Smithsonian

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This is the November 6, 2026 edition of True Colors, a look inside the global deals and colorful critiques that make the art world go round, with a weekly guide to the must-see openings, auctions, and fairs.

It’s True Colors. The Democrats have flipped the House and the Senate, which could usher in a new era of funding at the Smithsonian—or, at the very least, a vibe shift in DC. There are a ton of gallery shows opening around New York, and on the West Coast, LACMA Art+Film Gala goes down tomorrow night. We got the rare chance to sit down with one of the night’s honorees, Vija Celmins.

Mentioned in this issue: Donald Trump, Lonnie Bunch, Alma Allen, Tilman Fertitta, Yoshitomo Nara, Loïc Gouzer, Vija Celmins, Matthew Marks, Leonardo DiCaprio, Eva Chow, Denis Villeneuve, Gary Hume, Jacqueline Tran, Michael Govan, Katherine Ross, James Turrell, Riko Mizuno, Matthew Barney, Roni Horn, Meshell Ndegeocello, Steve McQueen, Matthieu Blazy, Rosemarie Trockel, Carrie Mae Weems, Marina Abramović, Wade Guyton, Christopher Wool, Rashid Johnson, Felix Rödder, Calvin Marcus, James Ensor.

Trump has spent the last two years attacking Washington’s cultural hub with executive orders and legally dubious firings issued via fiat. But the Smithsonian gets its funding through Congress—what if Congress actually acted on that authority?

Sure, Congress has more pressing issues to address once the deeply blue House and Senate get sworn in early next year. But here at True Colors I think it’s fair to ask the question: Will lawmakers wind back the years of impact the Trump administration has had on the Smithsonian Institution?

Although Congress has authority over the cultural behemoth, the White House has in just the last two years issued executive orders, been extremely hands-on in its public signage, and mounted pressure campaigns to fire directors and censor artworks. Congress could have stopped all of this, but didn't. Republicans naturally didn’t want to cross Trump and support the beleaguered arts mecca. But Democrats have every reason to—after the new Congress is sworn in they’ll have the power to appropriate massive amounts of funding to each Smithsonian museum—or even create new museums. Plus, that can impact how the whole institution is run on a day-to-day basis, as Congress has the power to shake up the Smithsonian’s board of regents. That governing body will choose the next Smithsonian secretary, after Lonnie Bunch resigned earlier this year.

I’m particularly interested in who takes over as the director of the Hirshhorn Museum and Sculpture Garden. Melissa Chiu, who held the position for 12 years, stepped down earlier this year to be the director of the Guggenheim Museum in New York, and rumors are swirling about who will succeed her in the post. The prospect of a culture-living Congress—and, who knows...a Democratic president come 2029?—could make the Smithsonian seem like a swell place to be long term.

Add in the prospect of a new crop of congressional staffers and the attendant cottage industries of consultants and lobbyists all flocking to Washington, and I could see the Hirshhorn getting caught up in the vibe shift, pronto. There’s expected to be a sea change of DC residents in town, and there’s every reason to believe they’ll make the (free! always free!) contemporary art museum a constant stop on the rotation. Plus, the long-gestating renovation of the Sculpture Garden literally just opened this past weekend. I don’t have any inside intel on who’s in the running for the job —but it’s looking like a pretty great gig these days.

Read True Colors: Inside the global deals and colorful critiques that make the art world go round, with a weekly guide to the must-see openings, auctions, and fairs.

Meanwhile, the artworks that filled the American Pavilion at the Venice Biennale, made by State Department–tapped artist Alma Allen, might be stuck in Italy for the foreseeable future. Funding has run out, and there’s nothing in the budget for moving the works. I wonder how that can be resolved. Seems like a job for an art-loving, restaurant-owning Italian ambassador, Tilman Fertitta!

The massive two-location Yoshitomo Nara show opened at David Zwirner last night, truly one of the great gallery moments here in New York this season. Don’t miss the pop-up one-painting exhibition of Nara’s In the Milky Lake / Thinking One, which will be sold by Loïc Gouzer’s one-lot auction business, Fair Warning. It’s on view at 534 W. 22nd Street. The lot opens for live bidding November 19 and is expected to sell for the eye-popping sum of $10 million to $15 million.

You can also see Nara’s work in Washington, DC, where the aforementioned Hirshhorn just opened a grand survey of the artist timed to the inauguration of its sculpture garden. And if you want to check out some of Nara’s artworks in a very unexpected place, head to the Avenue A dive bar Niagara, where Nara drunkenly created a masterwork on the walls late one night after an opening at Pace. The bar wisely conserved the artwork behind glass, and it remains there nearly two decades later.

It’s not usually worth reporting on the toasts given at gala dinners here in the prized real estate that is this newspaper’s Table Hopping section, but the Dia Fall Night had some good honorary toast content! On Monday, Matthew Barney proved himself to be a very deep Roni Horn fan with his toast. Then Meshell Ndegeocello went super outside the box, honoring Steve McQueen by stepping in front of the podium and leading a band around the gala. The whole evening was like that. Chanel wasn’t just a sponsor—the brand’s creative director Matthieu Blazy, a true contemporary art head, chaired the whole thing alongside artists Rosemarie Trockel and Carrie Mae Weems. Marina Abramović was at my table; she told me she reads Vanity Fair every time she’s on a plane and appreciates how thick the issues have been lately. Marina, it’s an honor. Thank you for reading.

Matthew Marks hosted a party on Wednesday night for Wade Guyton’s excellent new show at Bridges, the best restaurant on Chatham Square. Fun fact: Christopher Wool’s old studio was right next to the restaurant some decades back.

On Election Day, as the results started coming in, Rashid Johnson opened a show of new work at Hauser & Wirth’s Wooster Street gallery, which has recently become my favorite of the mega-gallery’s dozen or so global outposts. It’s a throwback of sorts, a small space in the neighborhood that was once the city’s gallery nexus, with an excellent track record of great shows. The dinner was at the gallery’s restaurant, Manuela, right around the corner on Prince Street. There was caviar, and while it was served to celebrate Johnson, it happened to come out right as the news networks indicated the Democrats were going to flip a Senate seat in South Carolina. Spirits were high.

If you’re making your way to The Met to see “Krasner and Pollock: Past Continuous,” do stop by Rodder, the gallery founded by Felix Rödder, to see one of the most inspired artist pairings in the city right now. Calvin Marcus, the fanatically talented and free-wheeling Los Angeles painter, has new and recent paintings, drawings, and ceramics installed alongside work by the great Belgian weirdo James Ensor. The chemistry between these two artists from different centuries and continents is uncanny. The dinner for the show was held somewhere unusual: House of the Redeemer, the Episcopalian Church in a former Italian Renaissance Revival townhouse once owned by a Vanderbilt heiress.

Bitcoin and USDT attract modest premiums in Paraguay as millions face banking crisis

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ueno Bank's collapse has sparked a currency selloff and rising Google searches for Bitcoin, fueling debate over whether crypto offers an escape from banking failures.

Bitcoin and USDT have traded at premiums in Paraguay's peer-to-peer markets as a banking collapse unsettles the country's financial system.

The turmoil has coincided with a 10% surge in the dollar against the Paraguayan guaraní, growing public interest in Bitcoin and calls from crypto executives for alternatives to traditional banking.

Data from CriptoNoticias, which tracks Binance P2P prices, showed Bitcoin's indicative buying price at 498 million guaraníes on Nov. 6. Using an exchange rate of approximately 6,469 guaraníes per dollar, this is equivalent to $76,982.53 and represents a premium of roughly 1.7% over the international Bitcoin price of $75,716.

A review of Binance P2P advertisements on Nov. 5 found a median buying price of 6,399 guaraníes for USDT, about 4% above the previous day's central bank dollar reference rate. The gap narrowed sharply as the official exchange rate caught up with the currency's depreciation.

Meanwhile, Google Trends data showed rising interest in Bitcoin and the dollar across Paraguay.

Searches for Bitcoin began trending upward on Oct. 23 and peaked on Oct. 30, before the formal announcement of Ueno Bank's closure. Interest remained evident as of press time, while searches for the US dollar also increased amid the financial uncertainty.

The developments suggest growing public attention to alternative assets as the country's banking and currency markets face mounting pressure.

Paraguay's central bank initiated resolution proceedings against Ueno Bank on Nov. 2 after determining that the lender's solvency ratio had fallen below half the legal minimum.

The decision disrupted access to deposits for customers of the digital lender, which served nearly 3 million people, and raised concerns about approximately $850 million in public-sector deposits, including pension and healthcare funds.

The financial disruption quickly spread to the foreign-exchange market.

According to local media outlet ABC Color, the retail selling price of the dollar climbed from 5,900 guaraníes on Oct. 30 to 6,500 on Nov. 5, representing an appreciation of approximately 10.2%.

The currency movement coincided with growing scrutiny of President Santiago Peña, who previously held a stake in Grupo Vázquez, the business group controlling Ueno Bank.

Peña has denied wrongdoing and maintained that he sold his investment in March 2025. He offered his resignation on Nov. 5, but lawmakers continued impeachment proceedings the following day.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

In a public statement, Peña pledged to cooperate with investigators while urging authorities to prioritize payments to affected depositors.

“Payment to savers cannot be left pending on what happens to me…My priority was to push, within the law and in coordination with the responsible institutions, the measures needed for savers to recover their savings. Clarifying the facts and resolving the payments are obligations that must move forward at the same time. One cannot be an excuse to postpone the other.”

The central bank has since initiated payments through Paraguay's deposit-guarantee framework, starting with eligible customers holding up to 10 million guaraníes.

The crisis has prompted several prominent crypto industry executives to argue that decentralized assets could reduce savers' exposure to banking failures.

Emin Gün Sirer, founder of Avalanche developer Ava Labs, said the developments demonstrated the benefits of cryptocurrencies like BTC, which are held outside conventional financial institutions. He wrote on X:

“[Crypto] assets such as AVAX and BTC are decentralized and require no trust in third parties. No one can take or freeze your AVAX [or BTC] without your consent.”

Samson Mow, CEO of Bitcoin adoption firm JAN3, made a similar argument, suggesting that governments could reduce the disruption caused by banking crises by establishing Bitcoin as an alternative financial network.

“Bitcoin adoption at a nation-state level can mitigate these types of situations. If BTC is available to the people as a parallel rail, then systemic banking failures have far less impact.”

Mow also said JAN3 met with Paraguay's central bank several years earlier to discuss Bitcoin adoption and the risks of conventional financial infrastructure.

Aave founder and CEO Stani Kulechov joined the discussion, declaring that “Bitcoin matters now more than ever” in reference to Paraguay.

Their comments reflect growing industry efforts to position cryptocurrencies as alternatives during financial crises, particularly in countries facing banking restrictions and currency instability.

However, the market's next test will be whether local Bitcoin and stablecoin activity remains elevated as Paraguay's authorities return deposits and attempt to restore confidence in the banking system.

Millennial moms are facing pay cuts at exactly the moment they need to earn more

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Mothers rarely win back the pay they lose after a first child. Millennial women are having babies now, often without leave or day care to keep working

At 34, Alicia Adamczyk, senior editor of personal finance newsletter The Purse, said she'd reached the age when college-educated women see their raises peter out. She married later than women in past generations did, doesn't own a home yet, and is weighing whether to have children. Corinne Low, an economist at the Wharton School who studies gender and discrimination, explained to her that "women's earnings do tend to level off in their mid-30s."

"That's the pattern, that's the average," Low added, "but that's not destiny."

Women don't stall in their mid-30s because of their age. Men the same age keep pulling ahead of them. In 2010, women who were 25 to 34, including the oldest millennials, made about $93 for every $100 men their age earned per hour, according to Pew Research Center. Nine years later, their salaries fell nearly three times as far behind. Over those same years, many of them started caring for children or other relatives and cut back at work, while men their age didn't.

Women fall furthest behind after a first child. Millennial women are having their first children now, many of them while starting their careers over. They're cutting back at work in the same years they're trying to rebuild. The pay women give up in their 30s doesn't come back. Gen X $TWTR women hit their 30s a decade before millennials, and they still make about $80 for every $100 men their age earn.

American women in heterosexual relationships have their first child at about 29, on average, and within a year, they earn about a fifth less than they did before having their baby, according to a working paper by Census Bureau and RAND Corporation researchers, who tracked couples' earnings records from 2005 to 2021. By 34, Adamczyk's age, they barely win back any of the pay they lost. But their husbands keep getting the same raises they got before they became fathers, and as the years go by, more of the family's money comes from the husband.

Mothers cut their hours or leave their jobs when a first child arrives, even when they bring home more money than their husbands, according to the Census and RAND researchers.. Their husbands keep working the same hours and keep getting raises, and many of those families end up depending more on the fathers' pay than on the mothers'. Same-sex couples don't divide the work that way. Both parents keep working, and by the time their child starts school, the lower earner is often making more than before.

The researchers concluded that couples decide which parent cuts back by what they think mothers and fathers are supposed to do, not by who brings home the bigger paycheck. Harvard economist Claudia Goldin told Princeton University Press in 2021 that the mother usually becomes the parent the family can always reach. "Working mothers are 'on-call at home,'" Goldin said, "whereas working fathers are 'on-call at work.'"

Goldin says mothers wouldn't have to be the parent "on-call at home" if flexible hours and child care cost families less. "The route out of these inequities is to lower the cost of workplace flexibility and childcare," she said. Half of working parents don't even get paid time off from their jobs for a new child, a sick relative, or their own health, according to a Pew survey in March. When a baby arrives, one parent takes the time unpaid or leaves, and that parent is usually the mother.

When a child gets sick or day care closes early, mothers usually leave work to handle it. Goldin says they lose far more than missed hours. The best-paying jobs pay the most to workers available at any hour. "Doubling the time far more than doubles the earnings," she said. "That is what we call 'greedy work.'" Each time mothers leave work early, they fall further out of line for those raises. Fathers stay available and collect them. And when they become the biggest earners, their families have even more reason to keep the them at work the next time a child gets sick.

Some mothers eventually weigh what day care costs against what they now earn and decide to stay home. Low, the Wharton economist, told Adamczyk that the choice "is not about the spot price right now versus your wages right now," because by leaving the workforce, mothers give up the "career growth that might increase your work-life balance later."

Millennial mothers are choosing now between unpaid time off and leaving their jobs. Without paid leave, many more will quit, saving a few years of day care bills while giving up raises for the rest of their careers.

A New Hudson Valley Art Space Promises to Be As Accessible as It Is In Flux

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Less than two blocks from the Katonah train station stands a nondescript, gray two-story building on one of the main drags of this Hudson Valley town. Emblazoned in a kerned-out serif font, its name, Branchline, belies what awaits inside: a public-facing exhibition space for the Gochman Family Collection (GFC).

Established just five years ago, the GFC has already become one of the world’s leading collections of contemporary Indigenous art, with more than 850 works. Until now, works from the GFC have primarily hung in the New York apartment of Becky Gochman, the collection’s founder and custodian, and her family, though some 200 works are typically on loan to institutions around the world at any given time. The GFC has generously opened the home to museum groups, schools, and artists for tours and events as a way to bring more attention to Native artists, but the organization’s ambition has always been to make its holdings as accessible as possible. Related Articles Leah Ke Yi Zheng Joins Antenna Space, Pérez Art Museum Miami to Honor Nicolas Berggruen, and More: Industry Moves for October 28, 2026 Elisa Nuyten's Vega Foundation Is Quietly Reshaping the World of Moving-Image Art

“It’s one thing to articulate a mission, but what matters to me,” GFC creative director and curator Rachel Martin (Tlingít) said Thursday during a press preview, “is if I’m putting that language out into the world [then] I’m making sure that our organization is following that with action.” She added that she sees Branchline as a place for “bringing community together, which means making the Native community feel like they are very much a part of what the decision-making parts of this are, and then also inviting the community of Katonah to come in and learn about new artists that they might not have heard of.”

Anticipating that many visitors will arrive via Metro-North, the GFC named the space after the rail term “branch line,” or the place “where paths diverge and new paths begin,” as GFC director Laura Phipps put it. “We were excited about all the sort of ideas that ‘branch line’ conjures—both thinking about the metaphor of the train and the metaphor of trees—and what we can do to experiment and grow in this space.”

Free to the public, Branchline is taking its time to grow into its new digs, which will eventually total 10,000 square feet across four buildings. Friday evening’s inauguration will show off the first completed space, as well as plans for expansion in the coming months across an L-shaped plot that runs from the main building’s entrance on the Parkway to Katonah Avenue. The building houses a three-room gallery on the ground floor, two bedrooms upstairs for invited artists to determine how they want to spend the time, and a nearly operational ceramics studio downstairs.

Architectural renderings on view Thursday afternoon show that the building’s back door will open onto a small sculpture deck leading to a yet-to-be-built barn, where large-scale works will go on view. (The original barn was in such bad shape that it had to be torn down to make way for an exhibition space.) The barn will in turn lead to the Annex, which will take a more experimental approach (more on that later). From there, visitors will turn left onto a patio and eventually reach the back entrance of Twig, a retail store and café.

For now, visitors will get a taste of Branchline’s ambitions through a more traditional offering. Inaugurating the modest, nearly 2,000-square-foot gallery is “Line Break,” curated by GFC assistant curator Moonoka Begay (Ndee + Diné) with collection associate Nicole van Brugge. “Lines are a core facet of all visual language,” the exhibition’s introductory wall text begins, but Begay and van Brugge look past the formal qualities that a line and its breaks contribute to a work. Instead, they treat the line as a metaphor for lineage and bloodlines, borders and cartographic marks, horizons and sound barriers.

A tight show of 28 works by 18 Indigenous artists, “Line Break” offers visually compelling juxtapositions of artists from different generations working in a range of mediums and styles. Jaune Quick-to-See Smith’s The Spaniard (1988), a semi-abstract aerial view of the Flathead Indian Reservation in Montana, hangs next to Sara Flores’s Untitled (Shao Pei Maya Kené), 2024, a painting rendered in the geometric abstract visual language of the Shipibo-Conibo people, whose designs, according to an exhibition brochure, “map the community’s local worldview, environmental understanding, and cosmology.” Farther along are Teresa Baker’s Echoes (2024) and George Morrison’s Night Shadows. Red Rock Variation: Lake Superior Landscape (1994). Both are brightly colored abstract meditations on landscape made in very different mediums: acrylic, yarn, willow, and artificial sinew on AstroTurf for Baker; acrylic on canvas on board for Morrison.

Even the most seemingly formal works reveal their secrets as you delve deeper into how these artists use lines to make meaning. Across from the Quick-to-See Smith and Flores works are two pieces from Nadia Myre’s “Code of Construction” series, in which the artist arranges strung ceramic cylinders into squares depicting the ground-to-air distress signals for “Require Map and Compass” and “Not Understood.” Myre’s choice of medium draws on the tradition of wampum belts, used by Indigenous communities of the Northeast Woodlands to communicate political relationships. Myre’s work finds an analog in Faye HeavyShield’s the red line II (2025), a strand of red beads that the artist strung by hand. Here it is displayed in a coil, though its configuration is flexible—perhaps a metaphor countering the seeming immutability of shape, form, and even time.

The exhibition reaches its crescendo at its center with Raven Chacon’s Storm Pattern (2021–24), a handwoven wool textile accompanied by a multichannel sound piece whose low hum visitors have been hearing since entering the gallery. In 2016, Chacon noticed the sounds made by drones flying over the Oceti Sakowin camp at Standing Rock, where protesters had gathered to oppose the Dakota Access Pipeline. Ranging from loud and piercing to soft and soothing, the sounds of police surveillance drones mingled and merged with those of the protesters’ countersurveillance drones. In that harmonious dissonance, Chacon saw a score, which he recorded and also translated into the woven textile, charting the drones’ erratic flight patterns in a traditional Diné weaving style and structure.

Rounding out “Line Break” are two elements that will likely look drastically different in a few months. The GFC commissioned Matthew Kirk to make a site-specific work for an exterior wall of the building along an alleyway. Across three plywood panels, Kirk has collaged painted tar paper in various abstract forms and shapes. The work was already showing its ephemeral quality on Thursday morning, the paper having warped in the light rain.

A few steps down the alleyway is the Annex, which is still under construction: the plywood is exposed, and power cords run to jerry-rigged lightbulbs that illuminate its two floors. Rather than hide this rawness, the GFC team has decided to embrace it. For now, the Annex is home to a small show of drawings by Shuvinai Ashoona, who maps imagined scenes—ones that could perhaps one day come true—onto the Arctic landscape where she lives in Nunavut, Canada. The team acknowledged that it hasn’t quite figured out how the space will look in the near term, even once the build-out is complete. That will depend on how artists interpret a prompt that gives them the autonomy to do what they want.

“What GFC is doing is not trying to become an institution,” Martin said. “It’s trying to just meet where things are needed, and a space is definitely needed for art that is outside of the white box that you’re used to seeing, so that’s what we’re offering.”

Apple TV renews ‘Knife Edge: Chasing Michelin Stars’ for season three

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Award-winning culinary show ‘Knife Edge: Chasing Michelin Stars’ is getting a third season, in an announcement that came hours after its second season came to a close. Here are the details.

Just as ‘Awkwafina: The Unlikely Cook’ premiered with a two-episode combo, and ‘Knife Edge: Chasing Michelin Stars’ aired its second season finale, the company confirmed that the latter will return for an eight-episode third season.

‘Knife Edge: Chasing Michelin Stars’ is hosted by Topjaw’s Jesse Burgess and executive produced by Gordon Ramsay. Each season tells the story of several restaurants around the world as they compete to earn, retain, or increase their Michelin Star ratings.

The show premiered on Apple TV in 2025 to a perfect 100% Tomatometer score and has since been nominated for a BAFTA Television Award in the Factual Entertainment category, and won Best Popular Factual Programme at the 2026 Broadcast Digital Awards.

With eight episodes in each season, the show has taken viewers inside some of the world’s most acclaimed restaurants, including Brazil’s Evvai, the first in Latin America to earn three Michelin Stars, and Ynyshir, a two-star restaurant in rural Wales.

“What I love most about making ‘Knife Edge’ is that, however much time I spend in restaurants around the world, I’m constantly surprised by the extraordinary people and stories we discover. Behind every restaurant is a very personal journey, and for the chefs we follow, a Michelin Star can represent years of sacrifice, obsession and an enormous amount of hard work. It’s a privilege to be able to tell these stories, and I’m absolutely thrilled that we get to do it all again for a third season”

Google’s original Nest Doorbell is broken for many right now

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

An issue with Google’s original Nest Doorbell has left live view of the video feed broken for many owners, and there’s no word on a fix just yet.

Users are reporting widely in the past few days that Live View video feeds from Nest Doorbells are broken in the Google Home app. The issue is affecting specifically the first-gen Nest Doorbell, which is best known as “Nest Hello,” but Google has been referring to it as “Nest Doorbell (wired, 1st gen).”

The issue has popped up in several Reddit threads in recent weeks, but seems to have spread much more widely in the past few days based on nearly a dozen Google support threads we found.

Some users also report that some first-gen Nest cameras are also not working properly, but we couldn’t find nearly as many reports of this.

Notably, Google did roll out an update to older Nest hardware at the end of October.

Interestingly, affected users say that Nest Doorbell notifications on Nest Hub displays are still working properly. Some have deduced that Google’s switch to WebRTC may be the cause of the problem, but it’s hard to say for certain seeing as workarounds based on that theory don’t reliably work.

Outside of generic troubleshooting messages, Google has yet to say anything about a fix, or even confirm there’s an issue. But, based on the number of reports, it’s very obvious there’s a problem on Google’s end.

We’ll update this post as more details come to light but, for now, sound off in the comments below if you’re running into this issue.

1970 Oldsmobile 442 For Sale With 3,000 Miles Shown Is Ready To Daily With A 455 V8

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Among the uninitiated, the Oldsmobile 442 name may not be as recognizable of a muscle car nameplate as the Mustang, Camaro, or Charger, but car enthusiasts know that it's just as muscular a car as the big names. Early '70s 442 models, which came equipped with the 455 cubic-inch or 7.0-liter V8, were especially muscular, which Oldsmobile claimed made 370 horsepower, though that number is commonly believed to be a conservative rating intended to appease insurance companies.

This numbers-matching 1970 Oldsmobile 442 is an unreasonably clean example of its breed, complete with a handful of modernizations that would make it a rather fetching daily driver. It likely won't see daily-driver duty, though, since the miles are so low. It shows just 3,039 miles on its odometer, and it's listed for sale on Hemmings at an asking price of $59,900.

The aforementioned modernizations include a Vintage Air climate control system, a modern stereo hidden in the glove box, and a Tremec five-speed manual transmission, though the iconic original Muncie M-21 four-speed manual is included in the sale, as is an 8-track player.

So long as you can afford to keep it fueled, the odometer should be ignored. This 442 is just begging to be driven – it's been babied for long enough. Its legendary 455 block has been rebuilt and bored with high compression pistons, high-flow heads, a performance intake, and Holley carbs, so it's more than ready to lay down some rubber.

Thanks to that babying, its interior is mostly original. The white seat covers have been replaced along with the carpeting, but its dashboard and door panels are the same ones that were fitted to it from the factory. It's finished in Twilight Blue Metallic with a contrasting white interior and white decals, and the paint only has a few small chips. It's also fitted with an exceptional New York license plate that reads "IV IV II" and is currently located in Bayville, New York. It's priced a bit higher than other 442s, but its ultra-low mileage, fortified engine, and modern touches likely justify its price.

Grand Theft Auto 6 finally arrives on November 19, but not for us, no—the big day is only for gamers on PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X/S. We're not being completely excluded, though: We can't have GTA 6 in November, but we can take advantage of pre-release discounts on a bunch of older GTA games—deals that, ironically, don't appear to be available on consoles.

As noted by GamesRadar, there are four GTA games available in two releases: Grand Theft Auto 3, Vice City, and San Andreas bundled up in the GTA Trilogy for $19.79/£19.79/€19.79—67% off the regular price—and Grand Theft Auto 4: Complete Edition, which includes The Lost and Damned and The Ballad of Gay Tony expansions, for $6/£5.09/€6, a 70% discount.

Those aren't the deepest discounts ever seen, but Grand Theft Auto games typically don't venture into the 90% off territory we all enjoy so much—and according to Isthereanydeal.com, this is as low as the GTA Trilogy has ever gone, and close to the mark for GTA 4.

You may recall that the GTA Trilogy Definitive Edition was a mess when it launched in 2021, but it seems to have turned that frown upside-down since then: On Steam, where the trilogy arrived in 2023, Vice City and San Andreas both hold "very positive" ratings. GTA 3 is stuck at a "mixed" rating, which is unfortunate: Glancing at user reviews, it sounds like the game still isn't in perfect shape, but it's also suffering from the fact that it's 25 years old, and was the first 3D game in the series—some jank and roughness was there right from the start.

Grand Theft Auto 4: The Complete Edition, on the other hand, doesn't suffer from those shortcomings. It's getting pretty long in the tooth too—it came out in 2008—but represents a shift in design and tone that led the series into what it is today. If you're looking for just one older GTA game to horse around with, this is the one, especially given that it's less than half the cost of the trilogy.

As mentioned, these sale prices are currently only being offered on Steam: There's currently no sign of any of them on either the Xbox or PlayStation stores (Fun fact: there actually is no digital version of GTA 4 on the PS Store because Sony didn't start selling full-priced games online until well after 2008). Maybe it's just coincidence, but they do appear to be intentional pre-GTA 6 sales: The discounts are on until November 19, when Grand Theft Auto 6 launches—except on PC.

When Is An Airport Lounge Visit Not Worth It? Here’s How I Decide…

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It goes without saying that a lot about the passenger experience when traveling by air has changed over the decades, both for better and worse. I think perhaps one of the most fascinating transformations we’ve seen over the years involves airport lounges, and how their purpose has completely flipped.

I’d like to talk about that in a bit more detail in this post, and share my approach to using lounges. Let me emphasize that there are of course all kinds of lounges out there, from international first class lounges, to international business class lounges, to Priority Pass, membership, and credit card lounges. This post is primarily about lounges that fall into that last category, in terms of being pretty widely accessible.

Look, I’m not trying to sound like some boomer talking about how things were in “the good old days” (nothing against boomers… hi, dad!), but I think it’s interesting to compare how things were 15-20 years ago, to how they are today.

Back in the day, airport lounges — even the most basic ones — were primarily about escaping the hustle and bustle of the terminal, and having a quiet place from which to work, with free Wi-Fi and easy access to charging ports. At the time, free Wi-Fi and charging ports in terminals were incredibly rare. The food in most standard domestic lounges sucked — it was typically cubed cheese, snack mix, and not a whole lot else.

It also goes without saying that lounges were mostly very poorly monetized. Airport real estate is incredibly expensive, and lounges were primarily a cost center for their operators, as part of an overall value proposition for travelers.

Fast forward to today, and look at the state of lounges in the United States. Lounges have much better food than they used to, and they also have much better amenities than they used to. Also, exponentially more people have access to the lounges than they used to. They’re actually pretty nice destinations in airports.

The catch is that they’ve completely lost their initial purpose — they’re no longer a place you go to escape the crowds, and quite to the contrary, they’re often the most crowded place in a terminal. In many lounges you’ll find every single seat occupied, and even a very long waitlist to get in.

Obviously this is great for monetization, since lounge operators maximize their return by having the lounges utilized as much as possible, so that the cost can be divided by the highest number of people possible. Ironically, we’ve also seen terminals largely get much nicer over the years. It’s nowadays common to see free Wi-Fi and charging ports throughout terminals, and it’s usually not too hard to just find a quiet place to sit, with more peace than you’d find in a lounge.

Back in the day, if I had lounge access, my obvious move was always to use the lounge if I had time. I mean, that seems like a pretty obvious move, right? After all, you get access to something “premium,” so you want to make the most of it. Most people with TSA PreCheck don’t say “meh, you know, I think I’ll skip that today and go through the regular line.”

At the same time, I think one has to step back and look at things objectively. At times it almost feels like people use lounges religiously in order to justify the annual fees on their credit cards, and so they can talk about how they’re eating for “free.”

You’ll often see people on social media essentially call airport lounges upper middle class airport cafeterias, and that’s probably not actually too far off in terms of how they’re practically used… and there’s nothing wrong with that!

Personally, for places with consistently crowded lounges, my approach to lounge use is pretty straightforward:

Honestly, this is one of the reasons I really love grab & go lounges, where you can pick up something to take with you. To me that’s the ideal setup, since it serves the purpose I’m actually looking for from lounges — if a lounge is going to be crowded, I’d rather just be able to grab what I’d like, and head somewhere quiet.

As someone who loves coffee, it’s why I also appreciate when lounges have great coffee to go. It’s typically the item I most want to consume at airports, and the wait for a cup of coffee at an airport can often be quite long early in the morning.

The lounge crowding issue nowadays is only made worse by the number of people who are watching videos and making calls with no headphones in lounges.

The whole concept of airport lounges has changed massively over the years. Monetization of lounges has largely gotten better, as they’ve become a key component of the credit card race that we’re seeing between airlines and card issuers.

That’s a double edged sword. On the one hand, lounges have become higher quality, on average. On the other hand, demand for them has also increased massively.

For the most part, lounges are no longer a place you go to escape the bustle of the terminal, and to use free Wi-Fi and charge your electronics. Now they’re a place you go to eat, drink, and wait in line.

The way I’ve used lounges has definitely evolved over the years, and it’s one of the reasons I appreciate the grab & go concept that we’re increasingly seeing. Unless I’m hungry or thirsty, I prefer just finding a quiet gate area.

What do you make of how airport lounges have evolved, and has your approach to using them changed?

Doctors pull knife from man's eye that went through his brain—and he's fine now

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It’s the exact path, not the apparent severity that matters most, doctors say.

A man in his 20s showed up to a UK hospital with the hilt of a knife sticking out of his face. The 15 mm-wide blade hidden within went completely through his right eye and through his brain, with the tip of the blade hitting the top of his skull.

Despite the ghastly injury, the man was alive and conscious when he arrived. He could still talk and move all four limbs. His doctors described him as being in a state of agitation. On the Glasgow Coma Scale, he scored a 12 (E3 V4 M5), indicating a “moderate” brain injury, just one point above “mild.” He could open his left eye when told, but otherwise kept it closed (E3 score); he could talk and answer questions, but was confused (V4); and he intentionally moved away when pressure was applied to his body (M5).

In a report published Friday in BMJ Case Report, his doctors said they were concerned that in his confused, agitated state he may try to pull the knife out on his own. So, they swiftly sedated and intubated him. They put him on prophylactic treatments for bacterial infections, including tetanus. Then they used images to see what was going on in his head.

The blade was entirely through the right eye socket and eyeball. It traversed the left frontal and temporal lobes of his brain, passing through in front of—and without apparently injuring—the corpus callosum, which connects the two sides of the brain and is full of nerve fibers. The blade also missed the left internal capsule, which holds another bundle of nerve fibers.

In terms of damage to major blood vessels, doctors found that the blade was close to the left internal carotid artery, a major blood vessel, but didn’t seem to have damaged it. Doctors did find, however, that a branch of the middle cerebral artery looked to be indented by the knife.

After consulting with various medical specialties, the man’s doctors moved to emergency neurosurgery. They opted to remove part of his skull to directly see into the brain, but used a conservative “pull-and-see” method to slide the blade out rather than invasively excavate it. After it was out, the doctors saw no signs of significant hemorrhaging. The brain wound was cleaned and drained before surgeons patched up his skull. His right eye, however, couldn’t be saved. Given its extensive damage, it was removed.

The man then moved to intensive care, where he recovered rapidly. A day after surgery, doctors were able to remove his breathing tube. He developed some minor right-side weakness, but it resolved two days later. Scans of his brain showed steady improvement—a little bleeding and swelling, and there was some air in his brain tissue from where the knife had been. But otherwise, it appeared to be healing. He developed no infections and had no seizures.

Despite the “dramatic intracranial trajectory,” his language function was preserved, and he had no loss of movement or coordination, they reported. He was discharged from the hospital and at follow-up appointments remained “neurologically well,” with ongoing eye and mental health care.

As for how the injury happened, his doctors still don’t know. “The mechanism is unclear,” they wrote. However, they noted he had a history of depression.

The doctors reflected on how lucky the man was to have survived and to have such a full recovery. Such injuries that go through the eye and the brain are rare. When they do occur, they’re usually from “high-velocity mechanisms” such as gunshots or other ballistic injuries. Knives, rods, and nails fit into the rarer “low-velocity mechanisms. “Such “low-velocity penetrating brain injuries carry substantial morbidity and mortality because of hemorrhage, vascular injury, abscess, meningitis, cerebrospinal fluid leak, seizures and neurological deficit,” they wrote.

But such fortunate recoveries aren’t unheard of; they cited reports of other remarkably unexpected recoveries from seemingly devastating brain injuries. That includes the famous case of Phineas Gage, a railroad foreman who survived an 1848 accident that sent a large iron rod directly through his head. The rod entered below his left cheekbone and exited the top of his skull. (Although he survived and recovered, Gage’s personality was said to have changed after the accident. The doctors in the knife-wound case did not mention any such personality changes.)

“Echoing classic and contemporary examples of severe frontal lobe penetration with unexpectedly preserved neurological function, including Phineas Gage and a reported nail-gun injury, this case highlights that outcome may depend less on external severity than on precise trajectory relative to critical vascular, cortical and subcortical structures,” the doctors concluded.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.
